GITHUB ACTIONS
===============

1️⃣ WHAT IS GITHUB ACTIONS?
   → Workflow Automation Service by GitHub
   → Automates repository processes

2️⃣ REPOSITORY?
   → Container/bucket for code (apps, packages, websites)

3️⃣ TWO MAIN AUTOMATION AREAS:

   A) CI/CD (Most Important)
      CI = Continuous Integration
      CD = Continuous Delivery/Deployment

      Flow: Build → Test → Merge → Deploy

   B) Code Management
      → Code reviews, issue management

4️⃣ CI/CD EXPLAINED:

   Continuous Integration:
   • Auto-build code
   • Auto-test code
   • Auto-merge into codebase

   Continuous Delivery/Deployment:
   • Auto-publish new versions after testing

   DIAGRAM:
   Code Change → Build → Test → Merge → Deploy → Live ✅

5️⃣ REAL EXAMPLE:
   You change website code
   → GitHub Actions automatically tests it
   → If pass → Automatically publishes new version
   → Website updated! 🚀

6️⃣ OTHER AUTOMATIONS:
   ✓ Automated code reviews
   ✓ Issue management
   ✓ Repository maintenance

KEY POINT: GitHub Actions = Robot that automates build, test,
           deploy without manual work

GIT & GITHUB -
==============
1️⃣ WHAT IS GIT?
   → Free version control system tool
   → Download & install locally (Windows/macOS/Linux)
   → Manage source code changes for any language/app

    Note:-> "VCS = A System use to track & manage code changes"

2️⃣ GIT KEY FEATURES:
   • Commits = Snapshots of code at any point
   • Branches = Work on multiple versions simultaneously
   • Checkout = Switch between branches/snapshots
   • Rollback = Go back to older code if needed

3️⃣ GIT BENEFITS:
   ✓ Track code changes
   ✓ Recover from mistakes
   ✓ Develop features without breaking existing code

4️⃣ WHAT IS GITHUB?
   → Company offering cloud Git repositories
   → Cloud storage for your code + related services

   DIAGRAM:
   Local Git Repo ←→ GitHub Cloud Repo
   (Your machine)    (Cloud storage)

5️⃣ GITHUB FEATURES:
   • Public/Private repositories
   • Access control (who can modify)
   • Issues = Track bugs
   • Projects = Organize work
   • Pull Requests = Suggest code changes
   • Collaborative development tools

6️⃣ GITHUB vs GIT:
   GIT = Tool for version control (local)
   GITHUB = Cloud platform + collaboration features

7️⃣ GITHUB ACTIONS (in this context):
   → Automation service by GitHub
   → Automates repository & code processes
   → Enables CI/CD workflows

KEY POINT: Git manages code locally | GitHub stores +
           collaborates in cloud | GitHub Actions automates
===========================================================
GIT REPOSITORY -
--------------
1️⃣ WHAT IS GIT REPOSITORY?
   → Folder that Git uses to track code changes
   → Stores snapshots & metadata
   → Must exist to use Git commands

2️⃣ HOW TO CREATE?
   Command: git init
   → Run once per project
   → Creates hidden .git folder
   → Project now under Git control

3️⃣ BEFORE vs AFTER GIT INIT:

   BEFORE:
   Regular folder → Git commands don't work

   AFTER:
   git init runs → .git folder created → Git commands work ✓

4️⃣ KEY POINT:
   No Repository = Can't use Git
   Repository Created = Can use all Git features

5️⃣ AUTO-INITIALIZED PROJECTS:
   Some tools (React CLI, etc.) auto-create repo
   → Don't need git init again
==========================================================
GIT COMMITS -
-------------
1️⃣ WHAT IS A COMMIT?
   → Code snapshot at a specific point in time
   → Created after meaningful changes
   → Has unique ID & message

2️⃣ TWO-STEP PROCESS:

   STEP 1: git add <filename>
   → Stage files you want in snapshot
   → Choose which changes to include

   STEP 2: git commit -m "message"
   → Create snapshot from staged changes
   → Requires descriptive message

3️⃣ WHY TWO STEPS?
   → Control which files go in which commit
   → Don't have to commit everything at once

4️⃣ COMMON COMMANDS:

   git add index.html          → Stage file
   git status                  → Check staged files
   git commit -m "msg"         → Create commit
   git log                     → View all commits

5️⃣ WORKFLOW:

   Code Changes → git add → git commit → Snapshot Created ✓

6️⃣ COMMIT MESSAGE:
   → Short & descriptive
   → Example: "initial commit", "fix bug"

7️⃣ GIT LOG:
   → Shows all commits in order
   → Displays commit ID & message
   → Lets you go back to any commit

KEY POINT: Commits = Project history | Can rollback anytime
============================================================
GIT HEAD & CHECKOUT -
---------------------

1️⃣ WHAT IS HEAD?
   → Pointer to currently loaded commit
   → Always points to latest by default

2️⃣ GIT CHECKOUT:

   git checkout <commit-id>
   → Load old commit temporarily
   → Safe - nothing deleted

   git checkout <branch-name>
   → Return to branch with all commits

3️⃣ WORKFLOW:
   Make Changes → git add → git commit
                                 ↓
                            HEAD moves to
                            new commit

4️⃣ WHY CHECKOUT OLD COMMITS?
   • Debug/Find bugs
   • Revert mistakes
   • Review code changes
   • Restore deleted files

5️⃣ COMPLETE TIMELINE:

   Commit-1 → Commit-2 → Commit-3
   (v1)       (v2)       (v3 - Latest)
                          HEAD ↑

   AFTER: git checkout Commit-1-ID

   Commit-1 ← Commit-2 → Commit-3
   HEAD ↑     (Hidden)   (Still safe)

   AFTER: git checkout main

   Commit-1 → Commit-2 → Commit-3
                         HEAD ↑

6️⃣ USE CASE DIAGRAM:

   Bug Found in v3
        │
        ▼
   Checkout v1 (Working) ✓
        │
        ▼
   Compare & Find Issue
        │
        ▼
   git checkout main
        │
        ▼
   Create Fix (New Commit)

KEY POINT: Checkout = Time travel for debugging | Safe always
--------------------------------------------------------------
DIAGRAM:

   Website Working ✓ → Bug Introduced ✗
   (Commit 1)        (Commit 2)
        ↑                  │
        └──────────────────┘

   Checkout Commit 1 → Find where bug started
   Fix it → New commit

KEY POINT: Go back to old commits = Debug, Review, Restore
--------------------------------------------------------------
==============================================================
GIT REVERT:-Create new undo commit | History preserved
----------

1️⃣ WHAT IS GIT REVERT?
   → Undo changes from specific commit
   → Creates NEW commit (doesn't delete old one)
   → Safe way to undo mistakes

2️⃣ COMMAND:

   git revert <commit-id>
   → Reverts that specific commit
   → Prompts for commit message
   → Creates undo commit automatically

3️⃣ KEY DIFFERENCE:

   MANUAL UNDO:
   Remove paragraph → Edit files → git add → git commit
   (More work)

   GIT REVERT:
   git revert <id> → Done ✓
   (Automatic)

4️⃣ HOW IT WORKS:

   Commit-1 (initial) → Commit-2 (added link) → Commit-3 (reverted)
   (paragraph gone)

   Old commits still exist, new undo commit created

5️⃣ TIMELINE DIAGRAM:

   BEFORE REVERT:

   Commit-1 → Commit-2 (paragraph added) → HEAD

   AFTER: git revert Commit-2-ID

   Commit-1 → Commit-2 → Commit-3 (paragraph removed)
                         HEAD ↑

6️⃣ BENEFITS:
   ✓ Automatic undo (no manual editing)
   ✓ Complex changes handled easily
   ✓ History preserved (can see what happened)
   ✓ Safe rollback

7️⃣ WORKFLOW:

   Made Mistake ✗
        │
        ▼
   git revert <commit-id>
        │
        ▼
   Enter message
        │
        ▼
   Undo commit created ✓
   (Original still visible in history)

KEY POINT: Revert = Create new undo commit | History preserved
================================================================
GIT RESET --HARD - INTERVIEW NOTES

1️⃣ WHAT IS GIT RESET --HARD?
   → Delete commits permanently
   → Go back to specific commit
   → All commits after that are GONE
   → Rewrites history

2️⃣ COMMAND:

   git reset --hard <commit-id>
   → Deletes all commits after specified commit
   → Cannot undo (data loss possible)
   → USE WITH CAUTION

3️⃣ REVERT vs RESET:

   GIT REVERT:
   • Creates new undo commit
   • Keeps history
   • Safe ✓

   GIT RESET --HARD:
   • Deletes commits permanently
   • Rewrites history
   • Risky ✗

4️⃣ TIMELINE DIAGRAM:

   BEFORE RESET:

   Commit-1 → Commit-2 → Commit-3 → Commit-4 → HEAD

   AFTER: git reset --hard Commit-1-ID

   Commit-1 → (Deleted) → (Deleted) → (Deleted)
   HEAD ↑

5️⃣ COMPLETE GIT COMMANDS SUMMARY:

   git init
   → Initialize repository

   git add <file>
   → Stage changes

   git commit -m "msg"
   → Create commit

   git status
   → Check staged files

   git log
   → View all commits

   git checkout <id>
   → Load old commit temporarily

   git revert <id>
   → Create undo commit (safe)

   git reset --hard <id>
   → Delete commits (dangerous)

6️⃣ WHEN TO USE:

   ✓ REVERT = Default choice (safe, keeps history)
   ✗ RESET --HARD = Only if absolutely sure

7️⃣ RISK DIAGRAM:

   Safe Route:
   git revert → New commit → History kept ✓

   Dangerous Route:
   git reset --hard → Data loss → Can't undo ✗

KEY POINT: Use REVERT by default | RESET only with caution
================================================================
GIT & .GITIGNORE - COMPLETE LEARNING GUIDE
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
KEY POINTS TO REMEMBER
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

✓ .gitignore location = Project ROOT
✓ .gitignore purpose = Exclude auto-generated files
✓ Once added = Auto-excluded from ALL commits forever
✓ Use "git add ." = Respects .gitignore automatically
✓ Check "git status" = Before every commit
✓ Add comments = Explain why files are ignored
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
PART 1: UNDERSTAND .GITIGNORE
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

1️⃣ WHAT IS .GITIGNORE?
   → Special file to exclude files/folders from commits
   → Keeps repository clean
   → Prevents auto-generated/personal files being shared

2️⃣ WHY DO YOU NEED IT?

   Example: Your project creates these files:

   __pycache__/
   └─ Function: Python auto-creates cache for faster imports
   └─ Use Case: Speed up code execution on same machine
   └─ Ignore? YES ✗ (recreated automatically on other machines)

   .idea/
   └─ Function: IDE stores editor settings, layout, plugins
   └─ Use Case: Personal editor configuration
   └─ Ignore? YES ✗ (don't share personal preferences)

   *.pyc
   └─ Function: Compiled Python bytecode files
   └─ Use Case: Machine-specific compiled version of .py
   └─ Ignore? YES ✗ (original .py file is sufficient)

   These are AUTO-GENERATED:
   ✗ Not needed in repository
   ✗ Clutters shared codebase
   ✗ Wastes storage space

3️⃣ HOW TO CREATE .GITIGNORE?

   Step 1: In project root, create file named:
           .gitignore

   Step 2: Add files to ignore:

           # Python cache directory
           # Auto-created by Python when imports run
           # Stores compiled bytecode for faster loading
           __pycache__/

           # IntelliJ IDEA IDE settings folder
           # Contains editor configuration, layout, plugins
           # Personal to each developer's machine
           .idea/

           # Python compiled bytecode files
           # Auto-generated from .py source files
           # Machine-specific, recreated automatically
           *.pyc

   Step 3: Save file

4️⃣ .GITIGNORE LOCATION:

   Network-Automation/
   │
   ├── .gitignore ← ALWAYS IN ROOT (top level)
   ├── update.py
   ├── VPN.py
   └── API_FLOW.txt

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
PART 2: YOUR PROJECT EXAMPLE
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━���━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

   ✗ __pycache__/       → Python cache

YOUR .GITIGNORE CONTENT WITH EXPLANATION:

   # Python cache directory
   # Function: Stores compiled bytecode for faster imports
   # Use Case: Speeds up code execution
   # Why ignore: Recreated automatically on any machine
   __pycache__/

   # IntelliJ IDEA IDE settings folder
   # Function: Stores editor layout, plugins, configuration
   # Use Case: Personal editor preferences
   # Why ignore: Each developer has different editor setup
   .idea/

   # Python compiled bytecode files
   # Function: Machine-specific compiled version of .py files
   # Use Case: Faster execution (Python uses these)
   # Why ignore: Original .py file is sufficient, recreated on import
   *.pyc

   ├── API_FLOW.txt ✓ (committed)
   ├── CICD.txt ✓ (committed)
   ├── bg2.jpg ✓ (committed)
   │
   ├── __pycache__/ ✗ (ignored - NOT committed)
   ├── .idea/ ✗ (ignored - NOT committed)
   └── *.pyc ✗ (ignored - NOT committed)

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
PART 3: HOW .GITIGNORE WORKS
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

WORKFLOW:

   Step 1: You modify files
           update.py (changed)
           VPN.py (changed)

   Step 2: Python auto-creates:
           __pycache__/ (cache for speed)
           .idea/ (IDE settings)
           *.pyc (compiled files)

   Step 3: .gitignore says "Ignore these 3!"
           __pycache__/
           .idea/
           *.pyc

   Step 4: Run command:
           git add .
           (Stages ALL files EXCEPT ignored ones)

   Step 5: Staged files:
           ✓ update.py
           ✓ VPN.py
           (Auto-generated files NOT staged)

   Step 6: Create commit:
           git commit -m "message"

   RESULT: Only code files committed ✓
           Auto-generated files excluded ✓

DIAGRAM:

   Your Changes + Auto-Generated Files
            │
            ├─ update.py ✓
            ├─ VPN.py ✓
            ├─ __pycache__/ ✗ (in .gitignore)
            ├─ .idea/ ✗ (in .gitignore)
            └─ *.pyc ✗ (in .gitignore)
            │
            ▼
   git add .
   (Git checks .gitignore)
            │
            ▼
   Staged:
   ✓ update.py
   ✓ VPN.py
   (Ignored files excluded automatically)
            │
            ▼
   git commit
            │
            ▼
   Clean Commit ✓
   (Only relevant files)

PART 4: GIT COMMANDS
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

INITIALIZE:

   git init
   → Create Git repository (do once per project)

CHECK STATUS:

   git status
   → See what changed/staged
   Output shows: Staged files, Unstaged files, Ignored files

STAGE FILES:

   git add <file>
   → Stage single file

   git add file1 file2 file3
   → Stage multiple files

   git add .
   → Stage ALL files (respects .gitignore) ← RECOMMENDED

   git add <folder>
   → Stage all files in folder

CREATE COMMIT:

   git commit -m "message"
   → Create snapshot with description

   Examples:
   git commit -m "initial commit"
   git commit -m "update VPN configuration"
   git commit -m "fix bug in update.py"

VIEW HISTORY:

   git log
   → Show all commits (newest first)

   git log --oneline
   → Show short version

NAVIGATE COMMITS:

   git checkout <commit-id>
   → Load old commit (temporary/debug)

   git checkout main
   → Return to latest commit

UNDO COMMITS:

   git revert <commit-id>
   → Create new undo commit (SAFE) ← USE THIS

   git reset --hard <commit-id>
   → Delete commits permanently (RISKY)

BRANCHES:

   git branch
   → List all branches

   git branch <name>
   → Create new branch

   git checkout -b <name>
   → Create & switch to new branch

   git checkout <name>
   → Switch to existing branch

   git merge <name>
   → Merge branch into current branch

GITHUB SYNC:

   git push origin <branch>
   → Send commits to GitHub

   git pull origin <branch>
   → Get commits from GitHub

PART 5: YOUR PROJECT WORKFLOW
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

STEP-BY-STEP FOR Network-Automation:

1. Initialize:
   $ git init

2. Create .gitignore (in project root):
   Create file: .gitignore

   Add:
   # Python cache
   __pycache__/

   # IDE settings
   .idea/

   # Compiled Python
   *.pyc

3. Check status:
   $ git status

4. Stage files:
   $ git add .

5. Verify staged:
   $ git status

6. First commit:
   $ git commit -m "initial commit: add Python scripts and docs"

7. Make changes (edit update.py, VPN.py):
   $ git status

8. Stage changes:
   $ git add .

9. Second commit:
   $ git commit -m "update: VPN configuration and API flow"

10. View history:
    $ git log

11. Send to GitHub:
    $ git push origin main

PART 6: COMMON GITIGNORE PATTERNS
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

For Python Projects:

   # Python cache (auto-created during imports)
   __pycache__/

   # Compiled Python bytecode
   *.pyc
   *.pyo

   # Environment variables (API keys, passwords)
   .env

   # Virtual environment folder
   venv/

For JavaScript/Node:

   # Dependencies (large, reinstalled via npm)
   node_modules/

   # Environment secrets
   .env

   # macOS system file
   .DS_Store

For IDEs:

   # VS Code settings (personal editor config)
   .vscode/

   # IntelliJ IDEA settings
   .idea/

   # Vim swap files
   *.swp

General:

   # Log files (debug output)
   *.log

   # Backup files
   *.bak
   *.backup

   # Temporary files
   *.tmp
   temp/

COMMAND SHORTCUTS:

git init              → Initialize repo
git add .             → Stage all (respects .gitignore)
git commit -m ""      → Create commit
git status            → Check status
git log               → View history
git push              → Send to GitHub
git pull              → Get from GitHub

===============================================================
GIT BRANCHES - COMPLETE NOTES (ORGANIZED)
-----------------------------------------
Question: What are Git branches?

Answer:
-"A branch is a pointer to a sequence of commits.
Git branches allow parallel development by creating
separate lines of commits that can later be merged
into the main branch.

-By default, a 'main' branch exists automatically
when you initialize a repository. The main branch
is typically developed by the core team and contains
stable, production-ready code.

-You can create new branches (like feature-1) for
developers to work on new features independently
without affecting the main codebase. Each branch
maintains its own commit history, though branches
can share common commits at the branching point.

-Multiple commits can exist in both branches, but
each branch can have unique commits developed by
its assigned developers. This isolation keeps the
main branch stable while feature development happens.

-When development on a branch is complete, you can
merge it back to main using 'git merge', which
combines all changes into a single timeline while
preserving the complete history.
-This enables team collaboration where different
developer teams work on different features
simultaneously without conflicts, keeping the main
branch production-ready and allowing independent
development teams to work in isolation."

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
BASIC CONCEPT
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

Commit:
   └─ Snapshot of code at specific point in time
   └─ Represents a version of project
   └─ Example: C1, C2, C3, C4

Branch:
   └─ Pointer to sequence of commits
   └─ Represents line of development
   └─ Example: main, feature-1, bug-fix

Relationship:
   commit = building block
   branch = container for commits

Example:
   main branch has: C1 → C2 → C3 → C4
   (4 commits organized in 1 branch)

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
DEFAULT BRANCH
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

When you create repository:

   $ git init

Git automatically creates:
   └─ "main" branch (default)

All commits go into main by default:
   └─ Unless you create new branch
   └─ No extra work needed
   └─ Happens automatically

Example timeline:

   $ git init
   └─ main branch created (empty)

   $ git commit -m "C1"
   └─ C1 added to main

   $ git commit -m "C2"
   └─ C2 added to main

   $ git commit -m "C3"
   └─ C3 added to main

   $ git commit -m "C4"
   └─ C4 added to main

Result:
   main: C1 → C2 → C3 → C4

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
DIAGRAM FROM IMAGE - DETAILED
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

Visual diagram:

                main
                 |
            commit1 (C1)
                 |
            commit2 (C2) ----------- git branch feature-1 ----------->
                 |                                            |
            commit3 (C3)                                  commit2
                 |                                            |
            commit4 (C4) <----------- git merge feature-1 ---- commit5
                 |                                            |
                 ↓                                            ↓

UNDERSTANDING EACH PART:

LEFT SIDE (main branch):
   C1 → C2 → C3 → C4

   What it represents:
   └─ Main branch timeline
   └─ 4 commits in order
   └─ Stable production code
   └─ Developed by main branch developers

TOP ARROW (Create branch):
   git branch feature-1

   What it does:
   └─ Creates new branch from C2
   └─ feature-1 starts at C2
   └─ Both branches share C1, C2
   └─ Separates feature development from main

RIGHT SIDE (feature-1 branch):
   C2 → C5

   What it represents:
   └─ Feature branch timeline
   └─ Started from C2
   └─ Added new commit C5
   └─ Developed by feature branch developers
   └─ Isolated from main branch work

BOTTOM ARROW (Merge branch):
   git merge feature-1

   What it does:
   └─ Takes C5 from feature-1
   └─ Adds to main after C4
   └─ Combines histories
   └─ Executed on main branch (where you want changes)

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
STEP-BY-STEP FLOW
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

STEP 1: Initial commits in main

   main: C1 → C2 → C3 → C4

   What happened:
   └─ Project started with C1
   └─ Added features (C2, C3)
   └─ Fixed bugs (C4)
   └─ All on main branch
   └─ Developed by main branch developers
   └─ C3, C4 are from main branch only

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

STEP 2: Create a new branch

   Command:
   $ git branch feature-1

   Who uses this command:
   └─ Developer who wants to create new feature branch
   └─ Developer currently on main branch

   What happens:
   └─ Git looks at current position (C2 in diagram)
   └─ Creates new pointer named "feature-1"
   └─ Points to same commit (C2)

   Result:
   main:       C1 → C2 → C3 → C4
               (main branch developers continue here)

   feature-1:  C1 → C2
               (feature developers will work here)

   Status:
   └─ Two branches exist
   └─ Both at same history (C1, C2)
   └─ Branches will diverge from C2
   └─ main branch = original development path
   └─ feature-1 branch = new isolated path

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

STEP 3: Two branches now exist

   main:
   C1 → C2 → C3 → C4
   (main branch commits - developed by main developers)

   feature-1:
   C1 → C2 (and will grow here)
   (feature branch commits - will be developed by feature developers)

   What this means:
   └─ Both share C1 and C2
   └─ After C2, development separate
   └─ main has C3, C4 (main branch developers work here)
   └─ feature-1 doesn't have C3, C4 yet
   └─ C3, C4 stay on main only
   └─ feature-1 will add its own commits

SEPARATE DEVELOPMENT DIAGRAM:

Time Point: After Step 3

Developer 1 (on main branch):        Developer 2 (on feature-1 branch):
│                                    │
├─ git checkout main                 ├─ git checkout feature-1
├─ (working on main)                 ├─ (working on feature)
│                                    │
└─ C1 → C2 → C3 → C4                 └─ C1 → C2 → C5 (will be created)
   (main branch)                        (feature branch)

KEY DIFFERENCE:

main branch:
   └─ Developers: Anyone on main branch
   └─ Commits: C1, C2, C3, C4
   └─ Work: Bug fixes, maintenance
   └─ Access: Everyone (production code)

feature-1 branch:
   └─ Developers: Feature team only
   └─ Commits: C1, C2, C5, C6... (will add)
   └─ Work: New payment feature
   └─ Access: Feature team (isolated)

SEPARATION EXPLANATION:

Why separate?
   └─ main branch = stable, tested, production ready
   └─ feature-1 = experimental, under development
   └─ Different developers work on different branches
   └─ No one interferes with production code
   └─ feature can be broken, main stays safe

How it stays separate?
   └─ Different branch pointers
   └─ main points to C4
   └─ feature-1 points to C2 (at this step)
   └─ When feature developers commit, only feature-1 advances
   └─ main branch stays at C4 until merge

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

STEP 4: Work independently

   Developer 1 on main:
   └─ Currently on main branch
   └─ $ git checkout main (confirms on main)
   └─ Continues normal work
   └─ Creates C3, C4
   └─ Stable, tested code
   └─ Fixes bugs, adds maintenance

   Developer 2 on feature-1:
   └─ Currently on feature-1 branch
   └─ $ git checkout feature-1 (switched to feature)
   └─ New feature development
   └─ Creates C5 (with new feature)
   └─ Independent from main
   └─ Adds payment processing

   Result:
   main:       C1 → C2 → C3 → C4
               (main branch only)

   feature-1:  C1 → C2 → C5 (new feature)
               (feature branch only)

   Status:
   └─ Different C5s (different commits, different purposes)
   └─ feature-1 isolated from main
   └─ No interference between developers
   └─ main stays stable
   └─ feature-1 can be messy during development
   └─ main developers don't see feature code
   └─ feature developers don't affect production

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

STEP 5: Merge branch

   Command:
   $ git merge feature-1

   Who executes this command:
   └─ Someone on main branch (usually lead/manager)
   └─ After feature is complete and tested
   └─ Brings feature-1 changes into main

   Prerequisites:
   └─ Must be on main branch first
   └─ $ git checkout main (switch to main)
   └─ Then $ git merge feature-1 (merge happens)

   What happens:
   └─ Git compares main (C4) with feature-1 (C5)
   └─ Finds common parent (C2)
   └─ Takes changes from C2→C3→C4 (main path)
   └─ Takes changes from C2→C5 (feature path)
   └─ Combines C3, C4 from main
   └─ Combines C5 from feature-1
   └─ Creates unified history
   └─ No conflicts (different areas edited)

   Result:
   main: C1 → C2 → C3 → C4 → C5 (merged)
   (all changes on main now)

   Status:
   └─ All changes on main
   └─ Both feature-1 and main combined
   └─ Ready for production
   └─ Complete codebase
   └─ Users get payment feature

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
IMPORTANT CONCEPTS
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

HEAD:
   └─ Pointer to current branch
   └─ Shows where you are working
   └─ Moves when you switch branches

Example:
   $ git checkout feature-1
   └─ HEAD moves to feature-1
   └─ You are now on feature-1 branch
   └─ Any commits go to feature-1

   $ git checkout main
   └─ HEAD moves to main
   └─ You are now on main branch
   └─ Any commits go to main

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

Shared commits:
   └─ C1, C2 exist in both branches
   └─ Same data, same ID
   └─ Not duplicated, just referenced
   └─ Branching point starts here

Example:
   main:       C1 → C2 → C3 → C4
               └─────┬─────┘
   feature-1:      C2 (same C2)
                   └→ C5

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

Independent commits:
   └─ C5 on feature-1 ≠ C3, C4 on main
   └─ Different code
   └─ Separate development paths
   └─ Different purposes

Example:
   feature-1 C5: Add payment processing
   main C3: Add database
   main C4: Fix login bug

   (All different, no conflict)
   (C3, C4 on main only - feature doesn't have them)
   (C5 on feature only - main doesn't have it yet)

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

Branch behavior:
   └─ Does NOT affect other branches until merge
   └─ Isolation: work safely
   └─ Protection: main stays stable

Example:
   feature-1 breaks? → main still works
   feature-1 deleted? → main unaffected
   feature-1 merged? → main gets changes

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
COMMANDS REFERENCE
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

Create branch:
   $ git branch feature-1
   └─ Creates new branch from current commit
   └─ Doesn't switch to it
   └─ You stay on current branch
   └─ New branch is for other developers

Switch branch:
   $ git checkout feature-1
   └─ Moves HEAD to feature-1
   └─ Updates working files
   └─ You can now work on feature-1
   └─ Commits will go to feature-1

Merge branch:
   $ git merge feature-1
   └─ Combines feature-1 into current branch
   └─ Must be on main branch first
   └─ Preserves all commits
   └─ Brings feature code to main

List branches:
   $ git branch
   └─ Shows all branches
   └─ * marks current branch
   └─ Helps you see all available branches

Delete branch:
   $ git branch -d feature-1
   └─ Removes branch pointer
   └─ Commits still exist in history
   └─ Usually done after merging

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
REAL LIFE EXAMPLE
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

Project: Website

main branch (production):
   └─ Stable working website
   └─ Users depend on this
   └─ Tested and verified
   └─ Commits: C1, C2, C3, C4
   └─ Developed by: Main team / DevOps

feature-1 branch (development):
   └─ New payment feature
   └─ Under development
   └─ Not ready for users
   └─ Commits: C1, C2, C5
   └─ Developed by: Payment feature team

Workflow:

Day 1-4:
   └─ Everyone works on main
   └─ Commits: C1, C2, C3, C4
   └─ All developers on main branch

Day 5:
   $ git branch feature-1
   └─ Create payment feature branch
   └─ Executed by: Feature team lead
   └─ Creates isolated space for feature team

Day 5-7:
   $ git checkout feature-1
   └─ Feature team switches to feature-1
   └─ Works on payment feature
   $ git commit -m "add payment form"
   └─ Creates C5 on feature-1
   └─ Main branch still at C4
   └─ Main team unaffected

Day 8:
   $ git checkout main
   └─ Feature lead switches back to main
   $ git merge feature-1
   └─ Payment feature added to main!
   └─ C5 now part of main
   └─ Main now has all changes

Day 9:
   └─ Website has payment feature
   └─ Users can use new feature
   └─ All code stable and tested

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
KEY POINTS SUMMARY
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

✓ Commit = Snapshot of code
✓ Branch = Pointer to commits
✓ main = Default branch (auto-created)
✓ feature branch = Isolated development space
✓ Shared commits = Both branches use same C1, C2
✓ Independent commits = C5 on feature ≠ C3 on main
✓ Merge = Combine branches together
✓ Multiple branches = Parallel development
✓ Safe = main stays stable while working
✓ HEAD = Pointer to current branch
✓ main branch = Developed by main team
✓ feature branch = Developed by feature team


━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
WORKFLOW SUMMARY
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━��━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

Typical workflow:

1. Create branch:
   git branch feature-1
   (Created by: Feature lead or manager)
   (Purpose: Create isolated space)

2. Switch to branch:
   git checkout feature-1
   (Done by: Feature developers)
   (Purpose: Start working on feature)

3. Make changes and commit:
   git add .
   git commit -m "feature work"
   (Done by: Feature developers)
   (Purpose: Add code to feature branch)

4. Switch back to main:
   git checkout main
   (Done by: Feature lead after feature complete)
   (Purpose: Prepare for merge)

5. Merge feature to main:
   git merge feature-1
   (Done by: Feature lead or maintainer)
   (Purpose: Bring feature to main)

6. Delete feature branch:
   git branch -d feature-1
   (Done by: Maintainer)
   (Purpose: Clean up)

Result: feature work now part of main!
(All users get the new feature)
====================================================
19. WORKING WITH BRANCHES:-
--------------------------
GIT BRANCHES - COMPLETE WORKFLOW GUIDE
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
KEY POINTS
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

✓ Dev 1 (Tech Lead) creates feature branch on main
✓ Dev 1 pushes to GitHub for others to access
✓ Dev 2 fetches & switches to feature branch
✓ Dev 2 works independently on feature
✓ Dev 2 creates pull request when done
✓ Dev 1 reviews & approves
✓ Dev 1 merges feature to main
✓ Dev 1 deletes feature branch
✓ Both devs end up on main with complete code

Benefits:
   ├─ Main branch always stable
   ├─ No conflicts between developers
   ├─ Code review before merge
   ├─ Easy to track changes
   ├─ Professional workflow
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
WHAT ARE GIT BRANCHES?
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

Branch = Separate line of development
   └─ Multiple developers work simultaneously
   └─ Main code stays stable
   └─ Features developed in isolation

Why use branches?
   └─ Dev 1: Works on main (production code)
   └─ Dev 2: Works on feature (new feature)
   └─ No conflicts
   └─ Easy to merge when done

Real example:
   main branch = Working website
   feature branch = Payment system (under development)

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
BASIC CONCEPTS
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

Commit:
   └─ Snapshot of code
   └─ Has ID (C1, C2, C3, C4)
   └─ Can't be changed, only viewed

Branch:
   └─ Pointer to commits
   └─ Has name (main, feature-payment, etc.)
   └─ Can be created, switched, deleted

Main Branch:
   └─ Production code
   └─ Always stable
   └─ Protected (needs approval to merge)

Feature Branch:
   └─ New feature development
   └─ Isolated from main
   └─ Can be broken during development

Merge:
   └─ Combine two branches
   └─ All commits from feature come to main

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
TEAM STRUCTURE
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

Dev 1 (Tech Lead):
   ├─ Works on: main branch
   ├─ Creates: feature branches
   ├─ Merges: code to main
   └─ Reviews: pull requests

Dev 2 (Feature Developer):
   ├─ Works on: feature branch
   ├─ Creates: commits on feature
   ├─ Pushes: code to GitHub
   └─ Creates: pull request

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
DIAGRAM 1: INITIAL STATE
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

GitHub:
   main: C1 → C2 → C3 → C4

Dev 1's laptop:
   main: C1 → C2 → C3 → C4
   ↑ (Dev 1 here)

Dev 2's laptop:
   main: C1 → C2 → C3 → C4
   ↑ (Dev 2 here)

Status: Both on main, no feature branch yet

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
STEP 1: CREATE FEATURE BRANCH
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

Dev 1 executes (on main laptop):
   $ git branch feature-payment
   (Creates locally)

   $ git push origin feature-payment
   (Pushes to GitHub)

DIAGRAM 1.1: Branch Created

GitHub:
   main: C1 → C2 → C3 → C4
   feature-payment: C1 → C2 → C3 → C4

Dev 1's laptop:
   main: C1 → C2 → C3 → C4 (Dev 1 still here)
   feature-payment: C1 → C2 → C3 → C4

Dev 2's laptop:
   main: C1 → C2 → C3 → C4
   (Doesn't see feature yet - need to fetch)

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
STEP 2: DEV 2 SWITCHES TO FEATURE
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

Dev 2 executes (on feature laptop):
   $ git fetch
   (Downloads branches from GitHub)

   $ git checkout feature-payment
   (Switches to feature branch)

DIAGRAM 2: Dev 2 Switches

Dev 2's laptop:
   feature-payment: C1 → C2 → C3 → C4
                                    ↑ (Dev 2 here now)
   main: C1 → C2 → C3 → C4

Dev 1's laptop:
   main: C1 → C2 → C3 → C4
   ↑ (Dev 1 still on main)

GitHub: (unchanged)
   main: C1 → C2 → C3 → C4
   feature-payment: C1 → C2 → C3 → C4

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
STEP 3: DEV 2 MAKES CHANGES
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

Dev 2 executes (on feature-payment branch):
   $ git add payment.js
   $ git commit -m "Add payment form"
   (Creates C5 locally)

   $ git push origin feature-payment
   (Pushes C5 to GitHub)

Then:
   $ git add payment-process.js
   $ git commit -m "Add payment processing"
   (Creates C6 locally)

   $ git push origin feature-payment
   (Pushes C6 to GitHub)

DIAGRAM 3: Dev 2 Adds Commits

Dev 2's laptop:
   feature-payment: C1→C2→C3→C4→C5→C6
                                      ↑ (Dev 2)

GitHub:
   feature-payment: C1→C2→C3→C4→C5→C6 ✓
   main: C1 → C2 → C3 → C4

Dev 1's laptop:
   main: C1 → C2 → C3 → C4
   ↑ (Dev 1 unaffected)

━━━━━━━━���━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
STEP 4: PARALLEL DEVELOPMENT (SAME TIME)
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

Dev 1 on main (same time as Dev 2):
   $ git add styles.css
   $ git commit -m "Fix footer bug"
   (Creates C5 on main - different from feature C5)

   $ git push origin main

DIAGRAM 4: Parallel Work

GitHub:
   main: C1→C2→C3→C4→C5(bug fix)
   feature: C1→C2→C3→C4→C5(payment)→C6
   (Different C5 on each branch!)

Dev 1's laptop:
   main: C1→C2→C3→C4→C5(bug fix)
   ↑

Dev 2's laptop:
   feature: C1→C2→C3→C4→C5(payment)→C6
   ↑

Status: Both working independently ✓

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
STEP 5: CREATE PULL REQUEST
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

Dev 2 creates PR (via GitHub):
   From: feature-payment
   To: main
   Title: "Add payment feature"
   Description: "Implements payment processing"

GitHub shows:
   Pull Request #1 OPEN
   Waiting for Dev 1 review

Dev 1 gets notification:
   "Dev 2 wants to merge feature-payment"

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
STEP 6: CODE REVIEW
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

Dev 1 reviews (on GitHub):
   ✓ Checks C5, C6 changes
   ✓ Verifies tests pass
   ✓ Approves PR
   ✓ Adds comment: "Looks good!"

GitHub PR Status: APPROVED ✓

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
STEP 7: MERGE TO MAIN
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

Dev 1 merges (on main laptop):
   $ git fetch
   $ git checkout main
   $ git merge feature-payment
   (Combines C5, C6 from feature)

   $ git push origin main
   (Pushes merged code)

DIAGRAM 5: After Merge

GitHub:
   main: C1→C2→C3→C4→C5(bug)→C5'(payment)→C6'
   (All changes combined!)

   main: C1→C2→C3→C4→C5(bug)→C5'→C6'
   ↑

   feature: C1→C2→C3→C4→C5→C6 (unchanged)
   main: C1 → C2 → C3 → C4 (old)

Status: Feature merged, payment system live ✓

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

Dev 1 cleans up (on main laptop):
   $ git branch -d feature-payment
   (Delete locally)

   $ git push origin --delete feature-payment
   (Delete from GitHub)

GitHub:
   main: C1→C2→C3→C4→C5(bug)→C5'→C6' ✓

Dev 1's laptop:
   main: C1→C2→C3→C4→C5→C5'→C6'
   ↑

   main: C1→C2→C3→C4→C5→C5'→C6'
   ↑

COMMAND REFERENCE
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

   git branch feature-payment
   git push origin feature-payment

   git fetch
   git checkout feature-payment

Work & Commit (Dev 2):
   git add <file>
   git commit -m "message"
   git push origin feature-payment

Create Pull Request (Dev 2):
   (Via GitHub - Click "New Pull Request")

Review & Approve (Dev 1):
   (Via GitHub - Click "Approve")

Merge & Push (Dev 1):
   git fetch
   git checkout main
   git merge feature-payment
   git push origin main

Cleanup (Dev 1):
   git branch -d feature-payment
   git push origin --delete feature-payment

Update Local (Dev 2):
   git fetch
   git checkout main
   git pull origin main

=======================================================
20.MERGING BRANCHES - DETAILED GUIDE
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
WHY BRANCHES?
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

main = Production (live website)
feature = Development (new feature)
Don't merge half-finished features
Keep main stable

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
ON FEATURE BRANCH - MAKE CHANGES
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

You are on: feature-restructure branch

Step 1: Edit files
   ├─ index.html (new structure + links)
   └─ styles.css (new styling)

Step 2: Stage & commit (on feature branch)
   $ git add .
   $ git commit -m "added links and restructured page"

   Creates C5 on feature branch

DIAGRAM - FEATURE BRANCH:

   Feature: C1→C2→C3→C4→C5 (NEW!)
                            ↑
                       (Your commit)

   Main: C1→C2→C3→C4 (unchanged)

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
ON MAIN BRANCH - BUG FIX
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

Switch to main:
   $ git checkout main

   (On main branch now)

Edit file:
   └─ index.html: change "Welcome" to "Welcome!"

Stage & commit (on main branch):
   $ git add .
   $ git commit -m "changed title"

   Creates different C5 on main branch

DIAGRAM - MAIN BRANCH:

   Main: C1→C2→C3→C4→C5 (bug fix!)
                        ↑
                   (Your commit)

   Feature: C1→C2→C3→C4→C5 (unchanged)

⚠️ IMPORTANT: Both branches have C5 but different content!
   └─ Main C5 = "Welcome!" (title change)
   └─ Feature C5 = "Welcome" + links + structure

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
VERIFY FEATURE UNCHANGED
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

Switch back to feature:
   $ git checkout feature-restructure

   (On feature-restructure now)

Check files:
   └─ NO exclamation mark (only on main)
   └─ Links + structure still there

Why? Branches are independent ✓

DIAGRAM - DIVERGENCE POINT:

   Feature: C1→C2→C3→C4→C5(links)
                             ↑
                        (feature's C5)

   Main: C1→C2→C3→C4→C5(title!)
                       ↑
                   (main's C5)

   Common parent: C4
   Divergence: Both created C5 independently

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
MERGE FEATURE TO MAIN - STEP BY STEP
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

Goal: Combine both branches into main

Step 1: Switch to main
   $ git checkout main

   (You're now on main branch)

Step 2: Merge feature into main
   $ git merge feature-restructure

   Git analyzes:
   ├─ Common ancestor: C4
   ├─ Main changes: "Welcome!" (title)
   ├─ Feature changes: links + structure
   └─ Conclusion: Can auto-merge ✓

DIAGRAM - MERGE PROCESS:

   BEFORE MERGE:
   Feature: C1→C2→C3→C4→C5(links)
   Main: C1→C2→C3→C4→C5(title!) ← HEAD here

   MERGE COMMAND: $ git merge feature-restructure

   Git asks: "What changed?"
   ├─ From C4 to Main C5: added "!"
   ├─ From C4 to Feature C5: added links + structure
   ├─ Both edit index.html but different parts
   └─ Solution: COMBINE BOTH ✓

DIAGRAM - AFTER MERGE:

   Main: C1→C2→C3→C4→C5(title!)→C5'(merged)
                                   ↑
                          (merge commit)

   Result in C5':
   ├─ "Welcome!" (from main's title change)
   ├─ Links (from feature's links)
   └─ Structure (from feature's structure)

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
MERGE COMMIT MESSAGE
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

After merge command, editor opens automatically

Default message (auto-generated):
   Merge branch 'feature-restructure' into 'main'

This message:
   └─ Describes what merge happened
   └─ Auto-created by Git
   └─ Safe to accept as-is

How to save (depends on editor):
   vim: type :wq (write and quit)
   nano: Ctrl+O then Ctrl+X

Output after saving:
   Merge made by the 'recursive' strategy.
   index.html | 25 +++++++++++++++++++++++
   styles.css | 10 ++++++++
   2 files changed, 35 insertions(+)

What this shows:
   ├─ Strategy: Git's merge algorithm
   ├─ index.html: 25 lines added
   ├─ styles.css: 10 lines added
   └─ Merge successful ✓

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
VERIFY MERGE SUCCEEDED
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

View history (on main):
   $ git log --oneline

Output shows:
   abc1234 (HEAD -> main) Merge branch 'feature-restructure'
   def5678 changed title
   ghi9012 added links and restructured page
   jkl3456 added logo and styling

What this means:
   ├─ Merge commit at top
   ├─ Both branches' commits visible
   ├─ "changed title" = main's C5
   ├─ "added links and restructured" = feature's C5
   └─ All history preserved ✓

Check actual files:
   (Open index.html in browser)

   Website now shows:
   ├─ "Welcome!" ✓ (from main)
   ├─ New links ✓ (from feature)
   └─ New structure ✓ (from feature)

Everything combined! ✓

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
FEATURE BRANCH AFTER MERGE
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

Important: We merged feature INTO main, NOT vice versa

Switch to feature:
   $ git checkout feature-restructure

   (On feature branch)

Check history:
   $ git log --oneline

Output:
   ghi9012 (HEAD -> feature-restructure) added links and restructured
   jkl3456 added logo and styling

   Note: NO "Welcome!" here

Why?
   └─ Merge was one-way: feature → main
   └─ Feature branch untouched
   └─ Only main has the merged commit

DIAGRAM - AFTER MERGE:

   Main (merged):
   C1→C2→C3→C4→C5(title!)→C5'(merged)
                              ↑

   Feature (unchanged):
   C1→C2→C3→C4→C5(links)
                  ↑

   Feature still has old content, no "Welcome!"

━━━━━━��━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
DELETE FEATURE BRANCH
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

Feature branch no longer needed (merged)

Switch to main first:
   $ git checkout main

   (Must be on different branch to delete)

Delete feature branch:
   $ git branch -d feature-restructure

Output:
   Deleted branch feature-restructure.

Verify deletion:
   $ git branch

   Output: * main (only main exists)

What happened:
   ├─ Branch pointer deleted
   ├��� Commits preserved in main
   ├─ All changes safe in main
   └─ Repository cleaned ✓

DIAGRAM - FINAL STATE:

   Main: C1→C2→C3→C4→C5(title!)→C5'(merged)

   Feature-restructure: DELETED ✗

   ✓ All changes safe in main
   ✓ Website ready for production

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
WHEN AUTO-MERGE WORKS
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

This example: Auto-merge successful ✓

Why it worked:
├─ Main edited: index.html title (line 5)
├─ Feature edited: index.html structure (line 20+)
├─ Different lines = Git combines both
└─ No manual conflict resolution needed

What Git did:
   1. Identified both C5 commits
   2. Found common parent C4
   3. Compared differences
   4. Merged non-overlapping changes
   5. Created C5' with both changes

Result:
   └─ "Welcome!" from main
   └─ Links + structure from feature
   └─ All combined in one file

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
WHEN CONFLICT HAPPENS
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

If both edited SAME lines (conflict):

   Main: Changed line 5
   Feature: Also changed line 5

   Result: CONFLICT ✗

   What Git shows:
   <<<<<<< HEAD
   Welcome!  (from main)
   =======
   Welcome   (from feature)
   >>>>>>> feature-restructure

How to resolve:
   └─ Manual: Choose which to keep
   └─ Edit file, remove conflict markers
   └─ Save and commit
   └─ Advanced topic (not covered here)

This example: NO conflicts (different lines) ✓

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
COMPLETE MERGE WORKFLOW
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

FEATURE DEVELOPER:
1. (On feature-restructure)
2. $ git add .
3. $ git commit -m "added links and restructured page"

MAIN DEVELOPER:
4. $ git checkout main
5. $ git add .
6. $ git commit -m "changed title"

MERGE (Tech Lead):
7. $ git checkout main
8. $ git merge feature-restructure
9. (Save merge message when prompted)

VERIFICATION:
10. $ git log --oneline (check history)

CLEANUP:
11. $ git checkout main
12. $ git branch -d feature-restructure

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━��━━
KEY MERGE CONCEPTS
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

Merge = Combining two branches

What happens:
├─ Git finds common parent (C4)
├─ Git identifies what changed in each branch
├─ Git tries to combine changes
├─ If no conflicts: Auto-merge ✓
└─ If conflicts: Manual resolve needed

Merge commit:
├─ New commit (C5')
├─ Has two parents (both C5s)
├─ Contains both changes
└─ Preserves history ✓

Feature branch after merge:
├─ Remains unchanged
├─ Still has old commits
├─ Can be deleted safely
└─ Changes preserved in main

One-way merge:
├─ We merged feature INTO main
├─ Feature branch untouched
├─ Could also merge main INTO feature
└─ But not needed here

Auto-merge requirement:
├─ Changes in different parts
├─ OR different files
├─ Git can apply both
└─ No manual intervention

Merge message:
├─ Auto-generated by Git
├─ Describes merge
├─ Can be custom
└─ Save with editor

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
REAL-WORLD MERGE SCENARIO
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

Team workflow:

Developer 1 (Main):
├─ Working on bug fixes
├─ Committing to main
└─ Website stable

Developer 2 (Feature):
├─ Working on new payment system
├─ Committing to feature-payment
└─ Takes 3 days to complete

Day 4:
├─ Feature complete
├─ Dev 1 reviews code
├─ Dev 1 merges to main
└─ Website gets payment system

Result:
├─ Bug fixes from main
├─ Payment system from feature
├─ Both live on website
└─ Users happy ✓

========================================================
21.GITHUB - INTRODUCTION & OVERVIEW
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
GIT vs GITHUB
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

GIT:
   └─ Version control system (works locally)

GITHUB:
   └─ Cloud service (stores Git repos online)

Relationship:
   Git = Engine
   GitHub = Cloud storage + extra features

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
PROBLEMS WITH LOCAL GIT ONLY
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

Problem 1: Data Loss
   └─ Computer crashes = all code lost ✗

Problem 2: Team Collaboration
   └─ No easy way to share code
   └─ Manual copy (USB sticks, email) ✗

Problem 3: Multiple Machines
   └─ Code only on one laptop
   └─ Hard to access on other devices ✗

Solution: GitHub (Cloud backup + sharing)

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
WHAT IS GITHUB?
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

Definition:
   └─ Cloud-based Git repository storage provider
   └─ Store code in cloud servers
   └─ Owned by Microsoft

Main Purpose:
   ├─ Store Git repositories remotely
   ├─ Backup your code
   ├─ Share code with others
   └─ Collaborate on projects

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
GITHUB CORE FEATURES
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

1. CLOUD STORAGE

What it does:
   ├─ Stores Git repos in cloud
   ├─ Automatic backup
   └─ Access from any device

Benefits:
   ├─ Code safe if computer crashes ✓
   ├─ Available from any device ✓
   ├─ Team can access code ✓
   └─ All commits preserved ✓

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

2. CODE MANAGEMENT & COLLABORATION

What it includes:
   ├─ Issues (bug tracking)
   ├─ Pull Requests (code review)
   ├─ Projects (task management)
   ├─ Discussions (team communication)
   └─ Branches (feature development)

Benefits:
   ├─ Code review before merge ✓
   ├─ Bug tracking ✓
   ├─ Team communication ✓
   └─ Project management ✓

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

3. AUTOMATION & CI/CD

What it includes:
   ├─ GitHub Actions (automated workflows)
   ├─ CI/CD pipelines (auto-test & deploy)
   ├─ GitHub Apps (integrations)
   └─ Webhooks (event automation)

Benefits:
   ├─ Auto-run tests on commit ✓
   ├─ Auto-deploy on merge ✓
   ├─ Auto-check code quality ✓
   └─ Less manual work ✓

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
LOCAL vs CLOUD WORKFLOW
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

BEFORE GITHUB:
   Your laptop → .git/ folder (only copy)
   Problem: Crash = all lost ✗

AFTER GITHUB:
   Your laptop → $ git push → GitHub Cloud
   Two copies = safe backup ✓

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
DIAGRAM: GITHUB ARCHITECTURE
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

Your Laptop (Local):          GitHub (Cloud):
┌──────────────────┐          ┌──────────────────┐
│ Git Repository   │ ←push→   │ Remote Repo      │
│ - Commits        │ ←pull→   │ - Commits        │
│ - Branches       │          │ - Branches       │
│ - History        │          │ - History        │
└──────────────────┘          │                  │
                              │ + Extra:         │
                              │ - Issues         │
                              │ - Pull Requests  │
                              │ - Actions        │
                              │ - Projects       │
                              └──────────────────┘

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
COMMON GITHUB TERMS
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

Repository (Repo):
   └─ Storage for Git project (code + history)

Remote:
   └─ GitHub repository (cloud version)

Push:
   └─ Upload commits from local to GitHub

Pull:
   └─ Download commits from GitHub to local

Clone:
   └─ Copy entire GitHub repo to local machine

Fork:
   └─ Copy someone else's repo to your account

Pull Request:
   └─ Request to merge code changes

Issue:
   └─ Bug report or feature request

Action:
   └─ Automated workflow/pipeline

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
GITHUB WORKFLOW EXAMPLE
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

Step 1: Create repo on GitHub
Step 2: Clone to local
   $ git clone https://github.com/user/repo

Step 3: Work locally
   $ git add .
   $ git commit -m "message"

Step 4: Push to GitHub
   $ git push origin main

Step 5: Team pulls latest
   $ git pull origin main

Step 6: Create pull request
   └─ Request code review

Step 7: GitHub Actions run automatically
   ├─ Tests run
   ├─ Checks pass
   └─ Auto-deploy

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
WHY GITHUB?
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

✓ Backup: Code safe in cloud
✓ Accessibility: Access from any device
✓ Team Collaboration: Share code easily
✓ Project Management: Track bugs & features
✓ Automation: Auto-test & deploy
✓ Professional: Industry standard (80M+ users)

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
GITHUB ALTERNATIVES
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

Other options:
   ├─ GitLab (similar features)
   ├─ Gitea (self-hosted)
   └─ Bitbucket (by Atlassian)

But GitHub is:
   └─ Most popular, most features, industry standard

This course: Focus on GitHub & GitHub Actions
====================================================
22.GITHUB REPOSITORIES - SETUP & CREATION
------------------------------------------
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
REPOSITORY VISIBILITY
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

Public Repository:
   └─ Everyone can VIEW code
   └─ Only you/team can EDIT code
   └─ Good for open-source projects

Private Repository:
   └─ Only you/invited users can VIEW code
   └─ Only you/team can EDIT code
   └─ Good for personal/company projects

Important:
   └─ Public/Private = WHO CAN SEE
   └─ EDIT permissions = separate setting
   └─ Never controlled by public/private toggle

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
GITHUB ACCOUNT SETUP
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

Step 1: Visit GitHub.com

Step 2: Sign Up
   ├─ Email address
   ├─ Password
   ├─ Username (must be unique)
   └─ Solve captcha

Step 3: Verify Account
   └─ Enter verification code from email

Step 4: Choose Plan
   ├─ Free plan (sufficient for beginners)
   ├─ Pro plan ($4/month)
   └─ Enterprise plan (for big teams)

Free plan includes:
   ✓ Public repositories
   ✓ Private repositories
   ✓ GitHub Actions
   ✓ Basic features

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
GITHUB DASHBOARD
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

After account creation you see:

Dashboard shows:
   ├─ Your repositories
   ├─ Trending repositories
   ├─ Activity feed
   └─ Learning resources

What you can do:
   ├─ Create new repository
   ├─ Create/edit profile
   ├─ Join organizations
   └─ Explore other projects

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
CREATE NEW REPOSITORY
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

Where to create:
   └─ Click "+" icon (top right)
   └─ Select "New repository"

Form fields:

Repository name:
   └─ Name your repo
   └─ Example: "my-website"

Description (optional):
   └─ Brief description
   └─ Example: "Personal portfolio website"

Visibility:
   ├─ Public (everyone sees)
   └─ Private (only invited users see)

Initialize with:
   ├─ README.md (documentation file)
   ├─ .gitignore (ignore files)
   └─ License (open-source license)

Click: "Create repository"

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
AFTER REPOSITORY CREATION
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

You get:

Repository URL:
   └─ https://github.com/username/repo-name

Clone URL:
   └─ https://github.com/username/repo-name.git

You can now:
   ├─ Clone to local ($ git clone URL)
   ├─ Push local repo here
   ├─ Invite collaborators
   ├─ Create issues
   └─ Set up GitHub Actions

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
CONNECTING LOCAL TO GITHUB
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

Option 1: Clone existing GitHub repo

   $ git clone https://github.com/username/repo.git

   Creates:
   └─ Local folder with GitHub repo

Option 2: Push existing local repo to GitHub

   $ git remote add origin https://github.com/username/repo.git
   $ git push -u origin main

   Connects:
   └─ Local repo to GitHub repo

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
GITHUB WORKFLOW
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

Local work:
   $ git add .
   $ git commit -m "message"

Upload to GitHub:
   $ git push origin main

Get latest from GitHub:
   $ git pull origin main

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
KEY POINTS
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

✓ GitHub repository = cloud storage bucket
✓ Public/Private = visibility control only
✓ Edit permissions = separate from visibility
✓ Free plan sufficient for beginners
✓ Clone or push to connect local to GitHub
✓ Use push/pull to sync code
====================================================
23.GITHUB REPOSITORY - CREATION & CONNECTION

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
CREATE REPOSITORY ON GITHUB
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

Click: "+" button → "New repository"

Fill form:
   ├─ Repository name (must be unique)
   ├─ Description (optional)
   ├─ Visibility: Public or Private
   └─ Add files: README, .gitignore, License (optional)

Click: "Create repository"

Result: Empty GitHub repo ready ✓

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
THREE WAYS TO CONNECT
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

OPTION 1: CREATE NEW REPO FROM SCRATCH

No local repo yet?

   $ echo "# project-name" >> README.md
   $ git init
   $ git add README.md
   $ git commit -m "first commit"
   $ git branch -M main
   $ git remote add origin https://github.com/user/repo.git
   $ git push -u origin main

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

OPTION 2: PUSH EXISTING LOCAL REPO (MOST COMMON)

Already have local repo?

   $ git remote add origin https://github.com/user/repo.git
   $ git branch -M main
   $ git push -u origin main

What happens:
   1. Connects local → GitHub
   2. Renames branch to "main"
   3. Uploads all commits

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

OPTION 3: CLONE FROM GITHUB

Want to download existing repo?

   $ git clone https://github.com/user/repo.git

Already connected & ready to work ✓

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
COMMAND BREAKDOWN - OPTION 2
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

Step 1: Add remote
   $ git remote add origin https://github.com/user/repo.git

   └─ Links local to GitHub
   └─ "origin" = GitHub repo name

Step 2: Rename branch
   $ git branch -M main

   └─ Renames to "main" (GitHub default)

Step 3: Push to GitHub
   $ git push -u origin main

   └─ Uploads commits
   └─ "-u" sets default for future pushes
   └─ Now $ git push works alone

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
ECHO COMMAND (In OPTION 1)
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

$ echo "# project-name" >> README.md

What it does:
   ├─ echo = print text
   ├─ "# project-name" = content (# = markdown heading)
   ├─ >> = append to file
   └─ README.md = file name (created if doesn't exist)

Result: README.md file created with heading ✓

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
AFTER CONNECTION
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

Local & GitHub synced:

Local:
   C1 → C2 → C3 → C4

GitHub:
   C1 → C2 → C3 → C4 ✓

Ongoing workflow:

Work locally:
   $ git add .
   $ git commit -m "message"

Upload to GitHub:
   $ git push origin main

Download from GitHub:
   $ git pull origin main

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
KEY TERMS
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

Remote: GitHub repository
Origin: Default remote name
Push: Upload to GitHub
Pull: Download from GitHub
Clone: Copy repo from GitHub
Sync: Local & GitHub same commits

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
SUMMARY
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

1. Create GitHub repository (web)
2. Connect local to GitHub (git remote add)
3. Push commits to GitHub (git push)
4. Now backed up & shareable ✓

Public repo:
   └─ Anyone can see
   └─ Only you can edit

Connection one-time:
   └─ After setup, just push/pull
====================================================
24.GIT PUSH - COMPLETE GUIDE WITH ARCHITECTURE

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
WHAT IS GIT PUSH?
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

git push = Upload commits from LOCAL to REMOTE (GitHub)

LOCAL:          GITHUB:
C1→C2→C3→C4     [empty]
  ↓ push
              → C1→C2→C3→C4 ✓

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
SCENARIO - SETUP PHASE
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

You have:
   ├─ Local Git repo with commits
   ├─ GitHub repo created (empty)
   └─ Connected with: $ git remote add origin URL

Now push commits to GitHub

BEFORE PUSH:

┌─────────────────────────────────────┐
│        LOCAL REPO                    │
│                                      │
│ main:    C1 → C2 → C3 → C4          │
│ feature: C1 → C2 → C5 → C6          │
│                                      │
│ Status: Has commits ✓                │
└─────────────────────────────────────┘
         NOT SYNCED ✗
┌─────────────────────────────────────┐
│      REMOTE REPO (GitHub)            │
│                                      │
│ origin/main:    [empty]              │
│ origin/feature: [empty]              │
│                                      │
│ Status: Empty ✗                      │
└─────────────────────────────────────┘

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
VERSION 1: EXPLICIT PUSH (ALWAYS WORKS)
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

Command:
   $ git push origin main

Means:
   ├─ push = upload commits
   ├─ origin = to this remote
   └─ main = from this branch

Works every time (no setup needed)

AFTER: $ git push origin main

┌���────────────────────────────────────┐
│        LOCAL REPO                    │
│                                      │
│ main:    C1 → C2 → C3 → C4          │
│          (synced) ✓                  │
│ feature: C1 → C2 → C5 → C6          │
│          (NOT synced) ✗              │
│                                      │
└─────────────────────────────────────┘
       PARTIALLY SYNCED
┌─────────────────────────────────────┐
│      REMOTE REPO (GitHub)            │
│                                      │
│ origin/main:    C1→C2→C3→C4 ✓        │
│ origin/feature: [empty]              │
│                                      │
└─────────────────────────────────────┘

Problem: Type "origin main" every time

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
VERSION 2: SHORT PUSH (NEEDS -u FLAG)
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

Command (first time):
   $ git push -u origin main

What -u flag does:
   ├─ Uploads commits to GitHub
   ├─ Sets origin as default remote
   ├─ Sets main as default branch
   └─ Saves as "upstream"

AFTER: $ git push -u origin main

┌─────────────────────────────────────┐
│        LOCAL REPO                    │
│                                      │
│ main:    C1 → C2 → C3 → C4          │
│          (tracked by origin/main)↓  │
│ feature: C1 → C2 → C5 → C6          │
│          (NOT synced) ✗              │
│                                      │
└─────────────────────────────────────┘
       PARTIALLY SYNCED
┌─────────────────────────────────────┐
│      REMOTE REPO (GitHub)            │
│                                      │
│ origin/main:    C1→C2→C3→C4 ✓        │
│ origin/feature: [empty]              │
│                                      │
│ (main branch now has upstream set)   │
└─────────────────────────────────────┘

Now $ git push works (no need for origin main)

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
COMPARISON: FIRST PUSH OPTIONS
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

Option 1: $ git push
   ✗ Error (no default set)

Option 2: $ git push origin main
   ✓ Works (explicit always works)

Option 3: $ git push -u origin main (RECOMMENDED)
   ✓ Works + sets default
   ✓ Future $ git push works

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
SCENARIO - ONGOING WORKFLOW
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

Day 1: Initial setup
   $ git push -u origin main

   Local main:    C1→C2→C3→C4
   Remote main:   C1→C2→C3→C4 ✓
   (upstream set)

Day 2: New commit on main
   $ git add .
   $ git commit -m "new feature" (creates C5)

   Local main:    C1→C2→C3→C4→C5
   Remote main:   C1→C2→C3→C4 (out of sync)

   $ git push (short - works because -u set)

   Local main:    C1→C2→C3→C4→C5 ✓
   Remote main:   C1→C2→C3→C4→C5 ✓

Day 3: Switch to feature branch
   $ git checkout feature
   $ git push (ERROR - no upstream for feature)

   Solution:
   $ git push -u origin feature (set upstream)

   Local feature:  C1→C2→C5→C6
   Remote feature: C1→C2→C5→C6 ✓
   (upstream set)

Day 4: New commit on feature
   $ git add .
   $ git commit -m "more work" (creates C7)
   $ git push (works - uses feature's upstream)

   Local feature:  C1→C2→C5→C6→C7 ✓
   Remote feature: C1→C2→C5→C6→C7 ✓

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
COMPLETE ARCHITECTURE DIAGRAM
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

AFTER ALL STEPS:

┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│           LOCAL REPO (Your Laptop)                │
│                                                   │
│ main:    C1→C2→C3→C4→C5                          │
│          (tracked by origin/main) ↓              │
│                                                   │
│ feature: C1→C2→C5→C6→C7                          │
│          (tracked by origin/feature) ↓           │
│                                                   │
│ (Both branches have upstream set)                │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
     ↑↓ $ git push/pull
┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│        REMOTE REPO (GitHub - Cloud)               │
│                                                   │
│ origin/main:    C1→C2→C3→C4→C5 ✓                 │
│ origin/feature: C1→C2→C5→C6→C7 ✓                 │
│                                                   │
│ (All branches synced and have upstream)          │
└──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
AFTER -u IS SET (SUBSEQUENT PUSHES)
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

On main branch:
   $ git push
   ✓ Works (uses saved origin/main)

   $ git push origin main
   ✓ Still works (explicit still valid)

On feature branch (after -u set):
   $ git push
   ✓ Works (uses saved origin/feature)

   $ git push origin feature
   ✓ Still works (explicit still valid)

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
MULTIPLE REMOTES (Why explicit needed)
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

You could have:
   $ git remote add origin https://github.com/user/repo1.git
   $ git remote add backup https://github.com/user/repo2.git
   $ git remote add upstream https://github.com/other/repo.git

Push to origin:
   $ git push origin main (explicit)

Push to backup:
   $ git push backup main (explicit)

Push to upstream:
   $ git push upstream main (explicit)

Each needs different command (multiple -u not possible)

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
VERIFY UPSTREAM IS SET
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

Check all remotes:
   $ git remote -v

Check upstream for each branch:
   $ git branch -vv

   Output:
   * main    abc1234 [origin/main] commit message
     feature xyz7890 [origin/feature] commit message

   Shows which branch tracks which remote ✓

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
BEST PRACTICE
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

First push on any branch:
   $ git push -u origin <branch>
   (Set upstream once)

All subsequent pushes on that branch:
   $ git push
   (Use short version)

When switching branches without -u set:
   $ git push origin <branch>
   (Use explicit)

When multiple remotes exist:
   $ git push <remote> <branch>
   (Always explicit)

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
SUMMARY
━━━━━━━━���━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

$ git push origin main:
   ✓ Always works (no setup needed)
   ✗ Must type remote & branch every time

$ git push (after -u flag):
   ✓ Shorter, saves typing
   ✓ Requires setup once with -u
   ✗ Only works for branches with -u set

$ git push -u origin main:
   ✓ Uploads commits
   ✓ Sets default for future pushes
   ✓ Recommended for first push

Architecture:
   ├─ LOCAL repo has branches (main, feature, etc.)
   ├─ REMOTE repo has same branches (origin/main, origin/feature)
   ├─ PUSH syncs LOCAL → REMOTE
   ├─ PULL syncs REMOTE → LOCAL
   └─ Upstream tracks which remote each branch uses
===============================================================
25.GIT PUSH WITH AUTHENTICATION (MANISH-GANVIR/network-automation)

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
PROBLEM
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

$ git push origin main → Permission denied

Why?
   └─ GitHub needs authentication

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
SOLUTION: 3 STEPS
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

Step 1: Add username to URL
   $ git remote set-url origin https://MANISH-GANVIR@github.com/MANISH-GANVIR/network-automation.git

Step 2: Create Personal Access Token
   GitHub → Settings → Developer settings
      → Personal access tokens → Generate token
   (Copy token)

Step 3: Push with token
   $ git push origin main
   (Paste token when prompted)

SUCCESS OUTPUT:
   using up to 8 threads
   Compressing objects: 100% (5/5), done.
   Writing objects: 100% (5/5), 14.89 KiB | 4.96 MiB/s, done.
   Total 5 (delta 3), reused 0 (delta 0), pack-reused 0
   remote: Resolving deltas: 100% (3/3), completed with 3 local objects.
   To https://github.com/MANISH-GANVIR/network-automation.git
      29958fa..43f3549  main -> main ✓

OUTPUT MEANING:
   ├─ using up to 8 threads = 8 CPU cores
   ├─ Compressing objects: 100% (5/5) = 5 commits compressed
   ├─ Writing objects: 14.89 KiB = upload size
   ├─ 4.96 MiB/s = upload speed
   ├─ Total 5 (delta 3) = 5 commits, 3 changes
   ├─ 29958fa..43f3549 = old hash → new hash
   └─ main → main = synced to GitHub ✓

Step 4: Set upstream (optional)
   $ git push -u origin main

Step 5: Future pushes
   $ git push
   (Token cached - automatic)

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
DO YOU NEED TO USE TOKEN EVERY TIME?
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

NO!

First push: Enter token once
After that: OS automatically caches it
Future pushes: Token automatically used (no re-entry)

HOW IT WORKS:

Day 1 - First push:
   $ git push origin main
   ↓
   Prompt: "Enter password: _"
   ↓
   You: Paste token
   ↓
   OS: Stores token in cache

Day 2 - New commit:
   $ git push origin main
   ↓
   No prompt ✓
   ↓
   Token automatically used
   ↓
   Push succeeds

Day 3+ - Ongoing:
   $ git push origin main
   ↓
   No prompt ✓
   ↓
   Token still cached
   ↓
   Push succeeds

HOW TO KNOW IF TOKEN IS CACHED:

No prompt appears:
   └─ Token is cached ✓

Prompt appears:
   └─ Token cache expired
   └─ Need to enter again

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
WHEN TOKEN EXPIRES
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

You set expiry on GitHub (e.g., 90 days)

After 90 days:
   $ git push origin main
   ↓
   Error: Token expired
   ↓
   Generate new token on GitHub
   ↓
   Push again
   ↓
   New token gets cached

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
TIMELINE EXAMPLE
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

Day 1: First push
   $ git push origin main
   (Enter token once)
   ✓ Push succeeds
   ✓ Token cached

Day 2-89: Regular pushes
   $ git push origin main
   (No token needed - cached)
   ✓ Always works

Day 90: Token expires
   $ git push origin main
   (New prompt appears)
   (Enter new token)
   ✓ Works again
   ✓ New token cached

Day 91+: Regular pushes
   $ git push origin main
   (No token needed - new one cached)
   ✓ Works automatically

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
SUMMARY
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

✓ First push: Enter token once
✓ Future pushes: Automatic (token cached by OS)
✓ Token expiry: Only needed if token expires (90 days)
✓ After expiry: Generate new token, enter once, cache again
✓ You don't type token every time
✓ OS remembers and uses it automatically
=======================================================
26.WORKING WITH MULTIPLE BRANCHES ON GITHUB

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
STEP 1: CREATE NEW FEATURE BRANCH
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

Command:
   $ git checkout -b feat-section

What it does:
   ├─ Creates new branch "feat-section"
   ├─ Switches to it immediately
   └─ Ready for feature development

Verify:
   $ git branch

   Output:
   * feat-section  (you are here)
     main

ARCHITECTURE:

   LOCAL REPO:

   main:        C1 → C2 → C3 → C4 → C5
   feat-section: C1 → C2 → C3 → C4 → C5 → (new) ↓

   GITHUB: (No change yet)

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
STEP 2: MAKE CHANGES & COMMIT
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

Edit file:
   (Modify index.html - add section)

Stage & commit:
   $ git add index.html
   $ git commit -m "added section"

ARCHITECTURE AFTER COMMIT:

   LOCAL REPO:

   main:        C1 → C2 → C3 → C4 → C5
   feat-section: C1 → C2 → C3 → C4 → C5 → C6 ↓

   GITHUB: (Still no change)

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
STEP 3: PUSH FEATURE BRANCH TO GITHUB
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

Command:
   $ git push -u origin feat-section

What it does:
   ├─ Uploads feat-section to GitHub
   ├─ Sets upstream for future convenience
   └─ GitHub creates origin/feat-section

GITHUB NOTIFICATION:
   ├─ Shows "Compare and pull request" button
   ├─ Branch dropdown shows both branches
   └─ Code browsable on GitHub

ARCHITECTURE AFTER PUSH:

   LOCAL REPO:

   main:        C1 → C2 → C3 → C4 → C5
   feat-section: C1 → C2 → C3 → C4 → C5 → C6

   ↓ push

   GITHUB:

   origin/main:        C1 → C2 → C3 → C4 → C5
   origin/feat-section: C1 → C2 → C3 → C4 → C5 → C6 ✓

VIEW ON GITHUB:
   └─ Switch branches via dropdown
   └─ feat-section shows section added ✓
   └─ main shows no section ✓

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
STEP 4: MERGE FEATURE INTO MAIN (LOCALLY)
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

Switch to main:
   $ git checkout main

Merge feature branch:
   $ git merge feat-section

Result:
   ✓ Changes merged automatically
   ✓ Fast-forward merge (no conflicts)
   ✓ No merge commit created
   ✓ Main now has all changes

ARCHITECTURE AFTER MERGE:

   LOCAL REPO:

   main:        C1 → C2 → C3 → C4 → C5 → C6 ✓
   feat-section: C1 → C2 → C3 → C4 → C5 → C6

   (Both point to same commits)

   GITHUB: (Still showing old data)

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
STEP 5: PUSH UPDATED MAIN TO GITHUB
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

Command:
   $ git push origin main

What it does:
   └─ Uploads merged main to GitHub

ARCHITECTURE AFTER PUSH:

   LOCAL REPO:

   main:        C1 → C2 → C3 → C4 → C5 → C6
   feat-section: C1 → C2 → C3 → C4 → C5 → C6

   ↓ push

   GITHUB:

   origin/main:        C1 → C2 → C3 → C4 → C5 → C6 ✓
   origin/feat-section: C1 → C2 → C3 → C4 → C5 → C6

VIEW ON GITHUB:
   └─ Main branch shows section ✓
   └─ Both branches have same code

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
STEP 6: DELETE FEATURE BRANCH ON GITHUB
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

On GitHub:
   1. Go to "View all branches"
   2. Find feat-section branch
   3. Click trash icon
   4. Branch deleted

ARCHITECTURE AFTER DELETION:

   LOCAL REPO:

   main:        C1 → C2 → C3 → C4 → C5 → C6
   feat-section: C1 → C2 → C3 → C4 → C5 → C6

   (Still exists locally)

   GITHUB:

   origin/main:        C1 → C2 → C3 → C4 → C5 → C6
   origin/feat-section: [deleted] ✗

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
STEP 7: DELETE FEATURE BRANCH LOCALLY
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

Command:
   $ git branch -d feat-section

Verify:
   $ git branch

   Output:
   * main
   (feat-section gone)

FINAL ARCHITECTURE:

   LOCAL REPO:

   main: C1 → C2 → C3 → C4 → C5 → C6
   (feat-section deleted)

   GITHUB:

   origin/main: C1 → C2 → C3 → C4 → C5 → C6
   (feat-section deleted)

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
QUICK COMMANDS REFERENCE
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

Create & switch to branch:
   $ git checkout -b feat-section

Make changes & commit:
   $ git add .
   $ git commit -m "message"

Push branch to GitHub:
   $ git push -u origin feat-section

Switch to main:
   $ git checkout main

Merge branch:
   $ git merge feat-section

Push main:
   $ git push origin main

Delete branch locally:
   $ git branch -d feat-section

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
INTERVIEW Q&A
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Q: Why create separate branch for features?
A: Keeps main branch stable
   ├─ Main = production code
   ├─ Feature branch = experiments
   ├─ Merge when tested
   └─ Easy rollback if needed

Q: What is fast-forward merge?
A: Git simply appends new commits
   └─ No merge commit created
   └─ No conflicts to resolve
   └─ Clean commit history

Q: When is merge commit created?
A: When there are conflicts
   └─ Overlapping changes
   └─ Manual resolution needed
   └─ Separate commit required

Q: Branch deleted on GitHub - lost forever?
A: No, still exists locally
   └─ Delete locally separately
   └─ Commits still in history

Q: Can I push while on feature branch?
A: Yes
   └─ $ git push -u origin feat-section
   └─ Creates remote feature branch

Q: Do I need upstream (-u) for features?
A: Optional but recommended
   └─ Future pushes: just $ git push
   └─ Without -u: $ git push origin feat-section

(Your learning outcomes here)
